People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. I haven’t noticed myself being jumpy over little things in a way that feels unusual.\n\nMaybe I’d have to pay more attention to it, but it’s not something I would’ve thought to bring up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on the lookout for potential dangers or threats?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe not “danger” exactly, but I do feel tense a lot, like I’m waiting for something else to go wrong.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, because I’m still functioning and doing my work, but there’s this heavy feeling I can’t really shake. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’ve just been having a hard time shaking this heaviness. It’s probably not as bad as some people deal with, but I feel scared and tense most of the day, like I’m waiting for something else to go wrong. And I keep blaming myself for not stopping what happened. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but most of the day I have this heavy feeling. It’s like a mix of fear and guilt, and I’m tense, waiting for something else to go wrong. And I keep going back to blaming myself for not stopping what happened. Does that count as sad?\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I still do some things I used to like, and sometimes they’re okay… but I haven’t really been paying attention to whether I’m enjoying them the same way. Maybe a little less? I don’t know if that’s the kind of answer you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sleeping due to racing thoughts or worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most nights, honestly. I don’t know if I’d call them “racing thoughts” exactly, but I lie there for an hour or two replaying things. And then even when I finally fall asleep, I wake up really easily, like at every little creak in the house… or sometimes from dreams where I hear him hit the ground again. I know that probably sounds like a lot, but… yeah, sleep hasn’t been great.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not anything I’ve really noticed, anyway. My clothes seem to fit about the same. I haven’t been checking the scale regularly, though, so I don’t want to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same thoughts or memories, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. Not constantly, but it does happen. Like I’ll be working, measuring a railing or something, and if I hear a ladder scrape, it can just… kind of throw me. Out of nowhere I can see my husband falling, like it’s happening right in front of me again. My stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nI try to get back to what I’m doing pretty quickly, so it’s not like I’m trying to dwell on it. But yes, I guess that would count, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of what happened?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I’m still working, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do my job or anything. I don’t know if I’d call them “racing thoughts” exactly, but I lie there for an hour or two replaying things. And then even when I finally fall asleep, I wake up really easily, like at every little creak in the house… or sometimes from dreams where I hear him hit the ground again. I know that probably sounds like a lot, but… yeah, sleep hasn’t been great.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not anything I’ve really noticed, anyway. My clothes seem to fit about the same. I haven’t been checking the scale regularly, though, so I don’t want to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same thoughts or memories, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. Not constantly, but it does happen. Like I’ll be working, measuring a railing or something, and if I hear a ladder scrape, it can just… kind of throw me. Out of nowhere I can see my husband falling, like it’s happening right in front of me again. My stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nI try to get back to what I’m doing pretty quickly, so it’s not like I’m trying to dwell on it. But yes, I guess that would count, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of what happened?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I’m still working, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do my job or anything. But I have started turning down jobs if they involve raised porches or second-story decks… I usually just say my schedule’s too full or pass them to someone else.\n\nAnd at home, I kind of… find reasons not to talk about the repair, or I avoid looking at the spot where it happened. It’s not that I’m trying to be difficult. I just feel better if I don’t have to go there, I guess. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding thoughts or memories of what happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, at least some. I try not to make a big thing of it, but if something brings it up, I’ll kind of steer away from it.\n\nLike with work, I’ve started passing on jobs with raised porches or second-story decks, or I’ll say my schedule’s too full. And at home I’ll find excuses not to talk about the repair, or I just don’t look over at the spot where it happened. I know that probably counts as avoiding it… I’m just trying to keep things moving, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by small things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really, I don’t think. I mean, everyone jumps if something loud happens, right? I haven’t noticed myself being jumpy over little things in a way that feels unusual.\n\nMaybe I’d have to pay more attention to it, but it’s not something I would’ve thought to bring up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on the lookout for potential dangers or threats?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe not “danger” exactly, but I do feel tense a lot, like I’m waiting for something else to go wrong.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, because I’m still functioning and doing my work, but there’s this heavy feeling I can’t really shake. Fear, I guess… and guilt too, like I should’ve been able to stop what happened somehow. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy feeling and tension?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say about four months now. Since my husband fell during that porch repair.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down on its own after a little time, and maybe it has a little, I don’t know… but that heavy, tense feeling has kind of been there since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about since your husband's accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I still care about the things I cared about… I’m just maybe not as quick to do them, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I haven’t really noticed some big change where I don’t enjoy anything anymore. It’s more that things take a little more effort sometimes. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself participating less in hobbies or activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t know if I’d call it “participating less” in a big way. I still do things when I can. I think I’ve just been… slower to say yes, or I put things off more than I used to.\n\nI’m not trying to avoid everything or anything like that. It’s just been harder to get myself started sometimes. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in activities or hobbies since your husband's accident?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed. I mean, I still like the things I like. Maybe I’ve been a little less on top of doing them, but I wouldn’t want to say I’ve lost interest exactly.\n\nI’m sorry, I know I’m kind of splitting hairs. I just don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don't enjoy or care about things you used to find important or interesting since your husband's accident?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d put it that strongly. I still care about the important things, and I still enjoy things when I actually do them. My stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nI try to get back to what I’m doing pretty quickly, so it’s not like I’m trying to dwell on it. But yes, I guess that would count, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of what happened?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I’m still working, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do my job or anything. But I have started turning down jobs if they involve raised porches or second-story decks… I usually just say my schedule’s too full or pass them to someone else.\n\nAnd at home, I kind of… find reasons not to talk about the repair, or I avoid looking at the spot where it happened. It’s not that I’m trying to be difficult. I just feel better if I don’t have to go there, I guess. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding thoughts or memories of what happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, at least some. I try not to make a big thing of it, but if something brings it up, I’ll kind of steer away from it.\n\nLike with work, I’ve started passing on jobs with raised porches or second-story decks, or I’ll say my schedule’s too full. And at home I’ll find excuses not to talk about the repair, or I just don’t look over at the spot where it happened. I know that probably counts as avoiding it… I’m just trying to keep things moving, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by small things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really, I don’t think. I mean, everyone jumps if something loud happens, right? I haven’t noticed myself being jumpy over little things in a way that feels unusual.\n\nMaybe I’d have to pay more attention to it, but it’s not something I would’ve thought to bring up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on the lookout for potential dangers or threats?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe not “danger” exactly, but I do feel tense a lot, like I’m waiting for something else to go wrong.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, because I’m still functioning and doing my work, but there’s this heavy feeling I can’t really shake. Fear, I guess… and guilt too, like I should’ve been able to stop what happened somehow. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy feeling and tension?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say about four months now. Since my husband fell during that porch repair.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down on its own after a little time, and maybe it has a little, I don’t know… but that heavy, tense feeling has kind of been there since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about since your husband's accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I still care about the things I cared about… I’m just maybe not as quick to do them, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I haven’t really noticed some big change where I don’t enjoy anything anymore. It’s more that things take a little more effort sometimes. It’s just been harder to get myself started sometimes. But yes, I guess that would count, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of what happened?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I’m still working, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do my job or anything. But I have started turning down jobs if they involve raised porches or second-story decks… I usually just say my schedule’s too full or pass them to someone else.\n\nAnd at home, I kind of… find reasons not to talk about the repair, or I avoid looking at the spot where it happened. It’s not that I’m trying to be difficult. I just feel better if I don’t have to go there, I guess. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding thoughts or memories of what happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, at least some. I try not to make a big thing of it, but if something brings it up, I’ll kind of steer away from it.\n\nLike with work, I’ve started passing on jobs with raised porches or second-story decks, or I’ll say my schedule’s too full. And at home I’ll find excuses not to talk about the repair, or I just don’t look over at the spot where it happened. I know that probably counts as avoiding it… I’m just trying to keep things moving, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by small things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really, I don’t think. I mean, everyone jumps if something loud happens, right? I haven’t noticed myself being jumpy over little things in a way that feels unusual.\n\nMaybe I’d have to pay more attention to it, but it’s not something I would’ve thought to bring up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on the lookout for potential dangers or threats?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe not “danger” exactly, but I do feel tense a lot, like I’m waiting for something else to go wrong.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, because I’m still functioning and doing my work, but there’s this heavy feeling I can’t really shake. Fear, I guess… and guilt too, like I should’ve been able to stop what happened somehow. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy feeling and tension?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say about four months now. Since my husband fell during that porch repair.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down on its own after a little time, and maybe it has a little, I don’t know… but that heavy, tense feeling has kind of been there since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about since your husband's accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I still care about the things I cared about… I’m just maybe not as quick to do them, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I haven’t really noticed some big change where I don’t enjoy anything anymore. It’s more that things take a little more effort sometimes. I haven’t been checking the scale regularly, though, so I don’t want to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same thoughts or memories, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. Not constantly, but it does happen. Like I’ll be working, measuring a railing or something, and if I hear a ladder scrape, it can just… kind of throw me. Out of nowhere I can see my husband falling, like it’s happening right in front of me again. My stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nI try to get back to what I’m doing pretty quickly, so it’s not like I’m trying to dwell on it. But yes, I guess that would count, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of what happened?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I’m still working, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do my job or anything. But I have started turning down jobs if they involve raised porches or second-story decks… I usually just say my schedule’s too full or pass them to someone else.\n\nAnd at home, I kind of… find reasons not to talk about the repair, or I avoid looking at the spot where it happened. It’s not that I’m trying to be difficult. I just feel better if I don’t have to go there, I guess. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding thoughts or memories of what happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, at least some. I try not to make a big thing of it, but if something brings it up, I’ll kind of steer away from it.\n\nLike with work, I’ve started passing on jobs with raised porches or second-story decks, or I’ll say my schedule’s too full. And at home I’ll find excuses not to talk about the repair, or I just don’t look over at the spot where it happened. I know that probably counts as avoiding it… I’m just trying to keep things moving, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by small things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really, I don’t think. I mean, everyone jumps if something loud happens, right? I haven’t noticed myself being jumpy over little things in a way that feels unusual.\n\nMaybe I’d have to pay more attention to it, but it’s not something I would’ve thought to bring up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on the lookout for potential dangers or threats?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe not “danger” exactly, but I do feel tense a lot, like I’m waiting for something else to go wrong.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, because I’m still functioning and doing my work, but there’s this heavy feeling I can’t really shake. Fear, I guess… and guilt too, like I should’ve been able to stop what happened somehow. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy feeling and tension?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say about four months now. Since my husband fell during that porch repair.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down on its own after a little time, and maybe it has a little, I don’t know… but that heavy, tense feeling has kind of been there since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about since your husband's accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I still care about the things I cared about… I’m just maybe not as quick to do them, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I haven’t really noticed some big change where I don’t enjoy anything anymore. It’s more that things take a little more effort sometimes. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself participating less in hobbies or activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t know if I’d call it “participating less” in a big way. I still do things when I can. I think I’ve just been… slower to say yes, or I put things off more than I used to.\n\nI’m not trying to avoid everything or anything like that. It’s just been harder to get myself started sometimes. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in activities or hobbies since your husband's accident?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed. I mean, I still like the things I like. Maybe I’ve been a little less on top of doing them, but I wouldn’t want to say I’ve lost interest exactly.\n\nI’m sorry, I know I’m kind of splitting hairs. I just don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don't enjoy or care about things you used to find important or interesting since your husband's accident?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d put it that strongly. I still care about the important things, and I still enjoy things when I actually do them. I guess I’ve maybe been a little more distracted or harder to get going, but not like I don’t care anymore.\n\nI hope that makes sense—I’m not trying to dodge the question, I just haven’t really noticed that kind of change.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don't enjoy or care about things you used to find important or interesting most of the day, nearly every day, since your husband's accident?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s been most of the day or nearly every day. That sounds stronger than what I’ve experienced. I still care about things, and when I do them I can enjoy them… I may just not be as quick to make time for them.\n\nI mean, if I’m understanding what you’re asking, I wouldn’t say that’s really been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don't enjoy things you used to find pleasurable since your husband's accident?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still enjoy things when I’m actually doing them. Maybe I’m not always in the mood right away, or I have to kind of push myself to start, but once I’m there it’s not like the pleasure is gone.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped enjoying things I used to like.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a significant decrease in the pleasure you get from activities you used to enjoy since your husband's accident?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it a significant decrease, no. I still get pleasure from things I used to enjoy… maybe I’m a little slower to decide to do them, but once I’m doing them, it feels mostly the same.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you can't find pleasure in things you used to enjoy, even when you're actually doing them, since your husband's accident?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. If I’m actually doing something I used to like, I can still feel some enjoyment from it. I wouldn’t say I can’t find pleasure in it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
